I lie in bed staring at the ceiling for an hour or two replaying the day at the clinic, and then I wake up two or three times a night worrying about missed messages or scheduling mistakes. Even if I'm in bed for enough hours, I wake up feeling like I've barely slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control or stop these worries once they start, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like muscle tension or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, once the worries start, it's like an endless loop that I just can't shut off, even when I try to distract myself with my photography. I've always been a very active person, busy with my community volunteering and art projects, but this constant, overwhelming anxiety is completely new to me since the reorganization started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this anxiety and physical tension persist even on weekends or during vacations when you are completely away from the clinic and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely persists even on weekends and when I am completely away from the office. Even when I try to relax at home or go to a museum, my body feels too revved up to unwind, and my mind quickly loops right back to worrying about what could go wrong at the clinic."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello. I'm glad you're here today. What brings you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Lately I can’t seem to focus at work—my mind keeps drifting back to everything that could go wrong with my job. It's really starting to concern me because I've always been someone who can stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry, and did it start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: It's been going on pretty steadily for about nine months now, ever since my clinic announced a major reorganization. It brought a lot of uncertainty about my future job role, and after several long-time coworkers left, I've been completely overwhelmed trying to cover multiple schedules and extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this constant worry is mostly focused on your job situation, or does it also spill over into other areas of your life, like your health, family, or everyday minor tasks?\n\nPatient: It definitely spills over into other areas. I've started worrying constantly about our finances and whether we'll be able to pay rent if my hours change. I also find myself obsessing over minor everyday things, like worrying that a simple typo in an email will snowball into a major disaster and get me fired.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been feeling persistently down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply depressed, but I've definitely stopped participating in the hobbies I usually love, like my photography, art workshops, and volunteering. I just feel too overwhelmed and tense to keep up with them, and my mind is so constantly preoccupied with work worries that I can't actually enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling tense, have you also been experiencing physical symptoms like trouble sleeping, feeling easily fatigued, or irritability during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been having a lot of trouble sleeping lately. I lie in bed staring at the ceiling for an hour or two replaying the day at the clinic, and then I wake up two or three times a night worrying about missed messages or scheduling mistakes. Even if I'm in bed for enough hours, I wake up feeling like I've barely slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control or stop these worries once they start, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like muscle tension or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, once the worries start, it's like an endless loop that I just can't shut off, even when I try to distract myself with my photography. On top of that, my body feels constantly revved up—I'm always clenching my shoulders, tapping my foot, and I feel far too tense to even relax on the couch when I get home.\n\nDoctor: Before this reorganization at your clinic, did you have a history of being a frequent worrier, or have you experienced similar periods of intense anxiety in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced this level of intense, uncontrollable worry in the past. I've always been a very active person, busy with my community volunteering and art projects, but this constant, overwhelming anxiety is completely new to me since the reorganization started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this anxiety and physical tension persist even on weekends or during vacations when you are completely away from the clinic and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely persists even on weekends and when I am completely away from the office. Even when I try to relax at home or go to a museum, my body feels too revved up to unwind, and my mind quickly loops right back to worrying about what could go wrong at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently started any new medications, increased your caffeine intake, or do you have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that could be causing these physical symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started any new medications, and my caffeine intake hasn't changed at all. I also don't have any known medical conditions, like thyroid issues, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Alongside your sleep difficulties, have you also been feeling easily fatigued or unusually irritable during the day over these past nine months?"

I also find myself obsessing over minor everyday things, like worrying that a simple typo in an email will snowball into a major disaster and get me fired.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been feeling persistently down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply depressed, but I've definitely stopped participating in the hobbies I usually love, like my photography, art workshops, and volunteering. I just feel too overwhelmed and tense to keep up with them, and my mind is so constantly preoccupied with work worries that I can't actually enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling tense, have you also been experiencing physical symptoms like trouble sleeping, feeling easily fatigued, or irritability during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been having a lot of trouble sleeping lately. I lie in bed staring at the ceiling for an hour or two replaying the day at the clinic, and then I wake up two or three times a night worrying about missed messages or scheduling mistakes. Even if I'm in bed for enough hours, I wake up feeling like I've barely slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control or stop these worries once they start, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like muscle tension or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, once the worries start, it's like an endless loop that I just can't shut off, even when I try to distract myself with my photography. On top of that, my body feels constantly revved up—I'm always clenching my shoulders, tapping my foot, and I feel far too tense to even relax on the couch when I get home.\n\nDoctor: Before this reorganization at your clinic, did you have a history of being a frequent worrier, or have you experienced similar periods of intense anxiety in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced this level of intense, uncontrollable worry in the past. I've always been a very active person, busy with my community volunteering and art projects, but this constant, overwhelming anxiety is completely new to me since the reorganization started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this anxiety and physical tension persist even on weekends or during vacations when you are completely away from the clinic and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely persists even on weekends and when I am completely away from the office. Even when I try to relax at home or go to a museum, my body feels too revved up to unwind, and my mind quickly loops right back to worrying about what could go wrong at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently started any new medications, increased your caffeine intake, or do you have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that could be causing these physical symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started any new medications, and my caffeine intake hasn't changed at all. I also don't have any known medical conditions, like thyroid issues, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Alongside your sleep difficulties, have you also been feeling easily fatigued or unusually irritable during the day over these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed feeling unusually irritable or fatigued during the day, other than just feeling tired from the lack of sleep. Aside from that general exhaustion, I wouldn't say I've felt particularly snappy or easily worn out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having trouble focusing at work. Have you found that this difficulty concentrating, or your mind going blank, has been happening on most days over the past six months?"
  }

Lately I can’t seem to focus at work—my mind keeps drifting back to everything that could go wrong with my job. It's really starting to concern me because I've always been someone who can stay on top of things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry, and did it start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: It's been going on pretty steadily for about nine months now, ever since my clinic announced a major reorganization. It brought a lot of uncertainty about my future job role, and after several long-time coworkers left, I've been completely overwhelmed trying to cover multiple schedules and extra work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this constant worry is mostly focused on your job situation, or does it also spill over into other areas of your life, like your health, family, or everyday minor tasks?\n\nPatient: It definitely spills over into other areas. I've started worrying constantly about our finances and whether we'll be able to pay rent if my hours change. I also find myself obsessing over minor everyday things, like worrying that a simple typo in an email will snowball into a major disaster and get me fired.\n\nDoctor: Alongside this constant worry, have you also been feeling persistently down, depressed, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel deeply depressed, but I've definitely stopped participating in the hobbies I usually love, like my photography, art workshops, and volunteering. I just feel too overwhelmed and tense to keep up with them, and my mind is so constantly preoccupied with work worries that I can't actually enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling tense, have you also been experiencing physical symptoms like trouble sleeping, feeling easily fatigued, or irritability during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been having a lot of trouble sleeping lately. I lie in bed staring at the ceiling for an hour or two replaying the day at the clinic, and then I wake up two or three times a night worrying about missed messages or scheduling mistakes. Even if I'm in bed for enough hours, I wake up feeling like I've barely slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control or stop these worries once they start, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like muscle tension or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, once the worries start, it's like an endless loop that I just can't shut off, even when I try to distract myself with my photography. On top of that, my body feels constantly revved up—I'm always clenching my shoulders, tapping my foot, and I feel far too tense to even relax on the couch when I get home.\n\nDoctor: Before this reorganization at your clinic, did you have a history of being a frequent worrier, or have you experienced similar periods of intense anxiety in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced this level of intense, uncontrollable worry in the past. I've always been a very active person, busy with my community volunteering and art projects, but this constant, overwhelming anxiety is completely new to me since the reorganization started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this anxiety and physical tension persist even on weekends or during vacations when you are completely away from the clinic and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely persists even on weekends and when I am completely away from the office. Even when I try to relax at home or go to a museum, my body feels too revved up to unwind, and my mind quickly loops right back to worrying about what could go wrong at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently started any new medications, increased your caffeine intake, or do you have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that could be causing these physical symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started any new medications, and my caffeine intake hasn't changed at all. I also don't have any known medical conditions, like thyroid issues, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Alongside your sleep difficulties, have you also been feeling easily fatigued or unusually irritable during the day over these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed feeling unusually irritable or fatigued during the day, other than just feeling tired from the lack of sleep. Aside from that general exhaustion, I wouldn't say I've felt particularly snappy or easily worn out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having trouble focusing at work. Have you found that this difficulty concentrating, or your mind going blank, has been happening on most days over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been happening almost every day for about nine months now, ever since the reorganization was first announced. My mind will just blank out or wander into \"what if\" scenarios while I'm reading an email or checking in a patient, so I constantly have to reread things or ask people to repeat themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself also worrying excessively about things completely unrelated to your job or finances, such as the health and safety of your family, minor household chores, or being late for appointments?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really find myself worrying about things like my family's health or household chores. My anxiety is heavily tied to my work situation, the clinic's reorganization, making scheduling errors, and how that might affect our rent and finances."

I just feel too overwhelmed and tense to keep up with them, and my mind is so constantly preoccupied with work worries that I can't actually enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling tense, have you also been experiencing physical symptoms like trouble sleeping, feeling easily fatigued, or irritability during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been having a lot of trouble sleeping lately. I lie in bed staring at the ceiling for an hour or two replaying the day at the clinic, and then I wake up two or three times a night worrying about missed messages or scheduling mistakes. Even if I'm in bed for enough hours, I wake up feeling like I've barely slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to control or stop these worries once they start, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like muscle tension or feeling constantly on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, once the worries start, it's like an endless loop that I just can't shut off, even when I try to distract myself with my photography. On top of that, my body feels constantly revved up—I'm always clenching my shoulders, tapping my foot, and I feel far too tense to even relax on the couch when I get home.\n\nDoctor: Before this reorganization at your clinic, did you have a history of being a frequent worrier, or have you experienced similar periods of intense anxiety in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced this level of intense, uncontrollable worry in the past. I've always been a very active person, busy with my community volunteering and art projects, but this constant, overwhelming anxiety is completely new to me since the reorganization started.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this anxiety and physical tension persist even on weekends or during vacations when you are completely away from the clinic and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely persists even on weekends and when I am completely away from the office. Even when I try to relax at home or go to a museum, my body feels too revved up to unwind, and my mind quickly loops right back to worrying about what could go wrong at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently started any new medications, increased your caffeine intake, or do you have any medical conditions like thyroid issues that could be causing these physical symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't started any new medications, and my caffeine intake hasn't changed at all. I also don't have any known medical conditions, like thyroid issues, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Alongside your sleep difficulties, have you also been feeling easily fatigued or unusually irritable during the day over these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed feeling unusually irritable or fatigued during the day, other than just feeling tired from the lack of sleep. Aside from that general exhaustion, I wouldn't say I've felt particularly snappy or easily worn out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having trouble focusing at work. Have you found that this difficulty concentrating, or your mind going blank, has been happening on most days over the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes, this has been happening almost every day for about nine months now, ever since the reorganization was first announced. My mind will just blank out or wander into \"what if\" scenarios while I'm reading an email or checking in a patient, so I constantly have to reread things or ask people to repeat themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself also worrying excessively about things completely unrelated to your job or finances, such as the health and safety of your family, minor household chores, or being late for appointments?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really find myself worrying about things like my family's health or household chores. My anxiety is heavily tied to my work situation, the clinic's reorganization, making scheduling errors, and how that might affect our rent and finances.\n\nDoctor: If the reorganization at your clinic was completely resolved and your job and finances felt secure again, do you think your anxiety would completely go away, or would you likely find other things to worry about?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure, because the situation at work is so overwhelming right now that it's hard to picture how I would feel if it were completely resolved. I haven't really thought about whether my worry would just shift to something else, since I'm just trying to get through each day."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
